Um, yes—well, I was sent by occupational health, mostly. I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.

But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.

I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.\n\nAnd then when I do finally eat, I tend to keep it… small. Controlled. I suppose that’s the word they used, or maybe I did. It just feels easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number, like in the lab when a reading fluctuates a bit and you’re not supposed to overinterpret it—though of course sometimes a tiny fluctuation is the whole point, depending on the assay.\n\nBut with my weight, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp panic. Like I’ve slipped, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. Eating feels tied to that, so yes… I do worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. That word feels very… imprecise, actually. In science we’d have to define “excessive” against some sort of baseline, wouldn’t we? Like excessive for me, or excessive for a normal meal, or excessive compared with what occupational health thinks people should eat during a workday?\n\nSometimes if I’ve gone a long time without eating, I might have something and then feel like it was too much afterward, even if—objectively, perhaps—it wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of deliberately making myself vomit after eating.\n\nI’ve felt nauseous before, but that can happen in the lab too—some of the fixatives, or even just strong smells, there was this one time with an incubator that had gone wrong and everyone pretended they were fine but we absolutely were not. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.\n\nIt’s more that occupational health sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or exhibition work if I’m helping with that, and then when I finally do eat, I keep it to something very small and controlled. Something I can account for, I suppose. So… yes, certain foods, or at least certain very limited sorts of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose “significant” depends on what you’re comparing it to. My clothes are looser, which is hard to ignore because lab coats hide quite a lot, but trousers don’t, really. And the numbers have been… drifting down.\n\nI know I should probably sound more concerned about that. Part of me is. But another part of me sees it as evidence that I’m staying focused, if that makes sense—like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Which sounds a bit strange now that I’ve said it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body and emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is very loud.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny amount, even something that could just be water or—sorry, I know from a biology point of view bodies fluctuate, I’m not completely irrational about the mechanisms—but it still feels like this sharp panic. Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.

It tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.

By the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.

But honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. That’s partly why occupational health got involved, I suppose. So a “normal amount” without needing to compensate or tighten things up afterward… no, that doesn’t really happen much.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yes—well, I was sent by occupational health, mostly. I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.\n\nAnd then when I do finally eat, I tend to keep it… small. Controlled. I suppose that’s the word they used, or maybe I did. It just feels easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number, like in the lab when a reading fluctuates a bit and you’re not supposed to overinterpret it—though of course sometimes a tiny fluctuation is the whole point, depending on the assay.\n\nBut with my weight, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp panic. Like I’ve slipped, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. Eating feels tied to that, so yes… I do worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. That word feels very… imprecise, actually. In science we’d have to define “excessive” against some sort of baseline, wouldn’t we? Like excessive for me, or excessive for a normal meal, or excessive compared with what occupational health thinks people should eat during a workday?\n\nSometimes if I’ve gone a long time without eating, I might have something and then feel like it was too much afterward, even if—objectively, perhaps—it wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of deliberately making myself vomit after eating.\n\nI’ve felt nauseous before, but that can happen in the lab too—some of the fixatives, or even just strong smells, there was this one time with an incubator that had gone wrong and everyone pretended they were fine but we absolutely were not. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.\n\nIt’s more that occupational health sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or exhibition work if I’m helping with that, and then when I finally do eat, I keep it to something very small and controlled. Something I can account for, I suppose. So… yes, certain foods, or at least certain very limited sorts of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose “significant” depends on what you’re comparing it to. My clothes are looser, which is hard to ignore because lab coats hide quite a lot, but trousers don’t, really. And the numbers have been… drifting down.\n\nI know I should probably sound more concerned about that. Part of me is. But another part of me sees it as evidence that I’m staying focused, if that makes sense—like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Which sounds a bit strange now that I’ve said it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body and emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is very loud.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny amount, even something that could just be water or—sorry, I know from a biology point of view bodies fluctuate, I’m not completely irrational about the mechanisms—but it still feels like this sharp panic. Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.\n\nIt tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.\n\nBy the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods where you eat a normal amount of food without feeling the need to restrict afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.\n\nBut honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way you mean, probably.

I mean, “large amount” is a bit hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s normal is probably not very reliable at the moment. Sometimes at conferences they put out those trays of pastries and fruit and everyone sort of hovers around them pretending they’re only there for coffee, and I’ll think about whether taking one thing looks strange or not taking one looks strange, which is exhausting and also ridiculous because no one is watching that closely.

But episodes where I suddenly eat a lot in a short time… I haven’t really noticed that as a regular thing. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yes—well, I was sent by occupational health, mostly. I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.\n\nAnd then when I do finally eat, I tend to keep it… small. Controlled. I suppose that’s the word they used, or maybe I did. It just feels easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number, like in the lab when a reading fluctuates a bit and you’re not supposed to overinterpret it—though of course sometimes a tiny fluctuation is the whole point, depending on the assay.\n\nBut with my weight, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp panic. Like I’ve slipped, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. Eating feels tied to that, so yes… I do worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. That word feels very… imprecise, actually. In science we’d have to define “excessive” against some sort of baseline, wouldn’t we? Like excessive for me, or excessive for a normal meal, or excessive compared with what occupational health thinks people should eat during a workday?\n\nSometimes if I’ve gone a long time without eating, I might have something and then feel like it was too much afterward, even if—objectively, perhaps—it wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of deliberately making myself vomit after eating.\n\nI’ve felt nauseous before, but that can happen in the lab too—some of the fixatives, or even just strong smells, there was this one time with an incubator that had gone wrong and everyone pretended they were fine but we absolutely were not. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.\n\nIt’s more that occupational health sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or exhibition work if I’m helping with that, and then when I finally do eat, I keep it to something very small and controlled. Something I can account for, I suppose. So… yes, certain foods, or at least certain very limited sorts of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose “significant” depends on what you’re comparing it to. My clothes are looser, which is hard to ignore because lab coats hide quite a lot, but trousers don’t, really. And the numbers have been… drifting down.\n\nI know I should probably sound more concerned about that. Part of me is. But another part of me sees it as evidence that I’m staying focused, if that makes sense—like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Which sounds a bit strange now that I’ve said it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body and emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is very loud.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny amount, even something that could just be water or—sorry, I know from a biology point of view bodies fluctuate, I’m not completely irrational about the mechanisms—but it still feels like this sharp panic. Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.\n\nIt tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.\n\nBy the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods where you eat a normal amount of food without feeling the need to restrict afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.\n\nBut honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. That’s partly why occupational health got involved, I suppose. So a “normal amount” without needing to compensate or tighten things up afterward… no, that doesn’t really happen much.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way you mean, probably.\n\nI mean, “large amount” is a bit hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s normal is probably not very reliable at the moment. Sometimes at conferences they put out those trays of pastries and fruit and everyone sort of hovers around them pretending they’re only there for coffee, and I’ll think about whether taking one thing looks strange or not taking one looks strange, which is exhausting and also ridiculous because no one is watching that closely.\n\nBut episodes where I suddenly eat a lot in a short time… I haven’t really noticed that as a regular thing. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / biological_scientist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"If the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic that I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose it is, though I don’t like saying it that plainly.

It’s not always some dramatic thought, like, “I can’t gain weight,” running through my head. It’s more immediate than that. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this sharp panic—like I’ve let something slip, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked very hard to keep. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud.

And then I start thinking about numbers, and whether the scale was on the same tile of the bathroom floor, because the tiles in my flat are slightly uneven—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yes—well, I was sent by occupational health, mostly. I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.\n\nAnd then when I do finally eat, I tend to keep it… small. Controlled. I suppose that’s the word they used, or maybe I did. It just feels easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number, like in the lab when a reading fluctuates a bit and you’re not supposed to overinterpret it—though of course sometimes a tiny fluctuation is the whole point, depending on the assay.\n\nBut with my weight, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp panic. Like I’ve slipped, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. Eating feels tied to that, so yes… I do worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. That word feels very… imprecise, actually. In science we’d have to define “excessive” against some sort of baseline, wouldn’t we? Like excessive for me, or excessive for a normal meal, or excessive compared with what occupational health thinks people should eat during a workday?\n\nSometimes if I’ve gone a long time without eating, I might have something and then feel like it was too much afterward, even if—objectively, perhaps—it wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of deliberately making myself vomit after eating.\n\nI’ve felt nauseous before, but that can happen in the lab too—some of the fixatives, or even just strong smells, there was this one time with an incubator that had gone wrong and everyone pretended they were fine but we absolutely were not. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.\n\nIt’s more that occupational health sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or exhibition work if I’m helping with that, and then when I finally do eat, I keep it to something very small and controlled. Something I can account for, I suppose. So… yes, certain foods, or at least certain very limited sorts of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose “significant” depends on what you’re comparing it to. My clothes are looser, which is hard to ignore because lab coats hide quite a lot, but trousers don’t, really. And the numbers have been… drifting down.\n\nI know I should probably sound more concerned about that. Part of me is. But another part of me sees it as evidence that I’m staying focused, if that makes sense—like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Which sounds a bit strange now that I’ve said it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body and emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is very loud.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny amount, even something that could just be water or—sorry, I know from a biology point of view bodies fluctuate, I’m not completely irrational about the mechanisms—but it still feels like this sharp panic. Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.\n\nIt tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.\n\nBy the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods where you eat a normal amount of food without feeling the need to restrict afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.\n\nBut honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. That’s partly why occupational health got involved, I suppose. So a “normal amount” without needing to compensate or tighten things up afterward… no, that doesn’t really happen much.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way you mean, probably.\n\nI mean, “large amount” is a bit hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s normal is probably not very reliable at the moment. Sometimes at conferences they put out those trays of pastries and fruit and everyone sort of hovers around them pretending they’re only there for coffee, and I’ll think about whether taking one thing looks strange or not taking one looks strange, which is exhausting and also ridiculous because no one is watching that closely.\n\nBut episodes where I suddenly eat a lot in a short time… I haven’t really noticed that as a regular thing. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is, though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIt’s not always some dramatic thought, like, “I can’t gain weight,” running through my head. It’s more immediate than that. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this sharp panic—like I’ve let something slip, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked very hard to keep. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about numbers, and whether the scale was on the same tile of the bathroom floor, because the tiles in my flat are slightly uneven—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any significant medical issues or health problems as a result of your weight loss?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / biological_scientist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition\nmanifestation: \"I know people are concerned, but I honestly think they’re overstating it because I’m still functioning, still doing my experiments, and I don’t feel like this is medically dangerous.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… people have said they’re worried, yes. Occupational health, a couple of colleagues in that very carefully polite way people use when they don’t want to offend you.

But I don’t really feel like it’s a medical issue, if that makes sense. I’m still going into the lab, still running experiments, still keeping track of things. Last week I was doing this ridiculous sequence where one sample had to be checked every—sorry, that’s not really relevant. I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.

So I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. It doesn’t feel dangerous to me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yes—well, I was sent by occupational health, mostly. I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.\n\nAnd then when I do finally eat, I tend to keep it… small. Controlled. I suppose that’s the word they used, or maybe I did. It just feels easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number, like in the lab when a reading fluctuates a bit and you’re not supposed to overinterpret it—though of course sometimes a tiny fluctuation is the whole point, depending on the assay.\n\nBut with my weight, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp panic. Like I’ve slipped, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. Eating feels tied to that, so yes… I do worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. That word feels very… imprecise, actually. In science we’d have to define “excessive” against some sort of baseline, wouldn’t we? Like excessive for me, or excessive for a normal meal, or excessive compared with what occupational health thinks people should eat during a workday?\n\nSometimes if I’ve gone a long time without eating, I might have something and then feel like it was too much afterward, even if—objectively, perhaps—it wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of deliberately making myself vomit after eating.\n\nI’ve felt nauseous before, but that can happen in the lab too—some of the fixatives, or even just strong smells, there was this one time with an incubator that had gone wrong and everyone pretended they were fine but we absolutely were not. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.\n\nIt’s more that occupational health sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or exhibition work if I’m helping with that, and then when I finally do eat, I keep it to something very small and controlled. Something I can account for, I suppose. So… yes, certain foods, or at least certain very limited sorts of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose “significant” depends on what you’re comparing it to. My clothes are looser, which is hard to ignore because lab coats hide quite a lot, but trousers don’t, really. And the numbers have been… drifting down.\n\nI know I should probably sound more concerned about that. Part of me is. But another part of me sees it as evidence that I’m staying focused, if that makes sense—like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Which sounds a bit strange now that I’ve said it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body and emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is very loud.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny amount, even something that could just be water or—sorry, I know from a biology point of view bodies fluctuate, I’m not completely irrational about the mechanisms—but it still feels like this sharp panic. Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.\n\nIt tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.\n\nBy the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods where you eat a normal amount of food without feeling the need to restrict afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.\n\nBut honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. That’s partly why occupational health got involved, I suppose. So a “normal amount” without needing to compensate or tighten things up afterward… no, that doesn’t really happen much.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way you mean, probably.\n\nI mean, “large amount” is a bit hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s normal is probably not very reliable at the moment. Sometimes at conferences they put out those trays of pastries and fruit and everyone sort of hovers around them pretending they’re only there for coffee, and I’ll think about whether taking one thing looks strange or not taking one looks strange, which is exhausting and also ridiculous because no one is watching that closely.\n\nBut episodes where I suddenly eat a lot in a short time… I haven’t really noticed that as a regular thing. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is, though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIt’s not always some dramatic thought, like, “I can’t gain weight,” running through my head. It’s more immediate than that. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this sharp panic—like I’ve let something slip, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked very hard to keep. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about numbers, and whether the scale was on the same tile of the bathroom floor, because the tiles in my flat are slightly uneven—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant medical issues or health problems as a result of your weight loss?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said they’re worried, yes. Occupational health, a couple of colleagues in that very carefully polite way people use when they don’t want to offend you.\n\nBut I don’t really feel like it’s a medical issue, if that makes sense. I’m still going into the lab, still running experiments, still keeping track of things. Last week I was doing this ridiculous sequence where one sample had to be checked every—sorry, that’s not really relevant. I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.\n\nSo I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. It doesn’t feel dangerous to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.\n\nSo I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. It doesn’t feel dangerous to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / biological_scientist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it as losing control, exactly.

There are times when I feel very… preoccupied, I suppose, like I’m negotiating with myself over every bite, but that’s not quite the same thing. “Out of control” makes me think of someone eating without being able to stop, and I don’t really have that experience, at least not that I can clearly identify.

Although it’s hard, because food situations can feel chaotic even when nothing dramatic is happening. Like at departmental lunches, everyone’s balancing plates and talking about grant deadlines, and I’m trying to work out whether I’ve taken too much or too little, and then I miss half the conversation. But that’s more anxiety and calculation than losing control, I think.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yes—well, I was sent by occupational health, mostly. I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.\n\nAnd then when I do finally eat, I tend to keep it… small. Controlled. I suppose that’s the word they used, or maybe I did. It just feels easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number, like in the lab when a reading fluctuates a bit and you’re not supposed to overinterpret it—though of course sometimes a tiny fluctuation is the whole point, depending on the assay.\n\nBut with my weight, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp panic. Like I’ve slipped, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. Eating feels tied to that, so yes… I do worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. That word feels very… imprecise, actually. In science we’d have to define “excessive” against some sort of baseline, wouldn’t we? Like excessive for me, or excessive for a normal meal, or excessive compared with what occupational health thinks people should eat during a workday?\n\nSometimes if I’ve gone a long time without eating, I might have something and then feel like it was too much afterward, even if—objectively, perhaps—it wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of deliberately making myself vomit after eating.\n\nI’ve felt nauseous before, but that can happen in the lab too—some of the fixatives, or even just strong smells, there was this one time with an incubator that had gone wrong and everyone pretended they were fine but we absolutely were not. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.\n\nIt’s more that occupational health sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or exhibition work if I’m helping with that, and then when I finally do eat, I keep it to something very small and controlled. Something I can account for, I suppose. So… yes, certain foods, or at least certain very limited sorts of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose “significant” depends on what you’re comparing it to. My clothes are looser, which is hard to ignore because lab coats hide quite a lot, but trousers don’t, really. And the numbers have been… drifting down.\n\nI know I should probably sound more concerned about that. Part of me is. But another part of me sees it as evidence that I’m staying focused, if that makes sense—like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Which sounds a bit strange now that I’ve said it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body and emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is very loud.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny amount, even something that could just be water or—sorry, I know from a biology point of view bodies fluctuate, I’m not completely irrational about the mechanisms—but it still feels like this sharp panic. Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.\n\nIt tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.\n\nBy the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods where you eat a normal amount of food without feeling the need to restrict afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.\n\nBut honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. That’s partly why occupational health got involved, I suppose. So a “normal amount” without needing to compensate or tighten things up afterward… no, that doesn’t really happen much.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way you mean, probably.\n\nI mean, “large amount” is a bit hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s normal is probably not very reliable at the moment. Sometimes at conferences they put out those trays of pastries and fruit and everyone sort of hovers around them pretending they’re only there for coffee, and I’ll think about whether taking one thing looks strange or not taking one looks strange, which is exhausting and also ridiculous because no one is watching that closely.\n\nBut episodes where I suddenly eat a lot in a short time… I haven’t really noticed that as a regular thing. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is, though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIt’s not always some dramatic thought, like, “I can’t gain weight,” running through my head. It’s more immediate than that. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this sharp panic—like I’ve let something slip, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked very hard to keep. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about numbers, and whether the scale was on the same tile of the bathroom floor, because the tiles in my flat are slightly uneven—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant medical issues or health problems as a result of your weight loss?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said they’re worried, yes. Occupational health, a couple of colleagues in that very carefully polite way people use when they don’t want to offend you.\n\nBut I don’t really feel like it’s a medical issue, if that makes sense. I’m still going into the lab, still running experiments, still keeping track of things. Last week I was doing this ridiculous sequence where one sample had to be checked every—sorry, that’s not really relevant. I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.\n\nSo I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. It doesn’t feel dangerous to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as losing control, exactly.\n\nThere are times when I feel very… preoccupied, I suppose, like I’m negotiating with myself over every bite, but that’s not quite the same thing. “Out of control” makes me think of someone eating without being able to stop, and I don’t really have that experience, at least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nAlthough it’s hard, because food situations can feel chaotic even when nothing dramatic is happening. Like at departmental lunches, everyone’s balancing plates and talking about grant deadlines, and I’m trying to work out whether I’ve taken too much or too little, and then I miss half the conversation. But that’s more anxiety and calculation than losing control, I think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Which sounds awful when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about numbers, and whether the scale was on the same tile of the bathroom floor, because the tiles in my flat are slightly uneven—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant medical issues or health problems as a result of your weight loss?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said they’re worried, yes. Occupational health, a couple of colleagues in that very carefully polite way people use when they don’t want to offend you.\n\nBut I don’t really feel like it’s a medical issue, if that makes sense. I’m still going into the lab, still running experiments, still keeping track of things. Last week I was doing this ridiculous sequence where one sample had to be checked every—sorry, that’s not really relevant. I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.\n\nSo I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. It doesn’t feel dangerous to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as losing control, exactly.\n\nThere are times when I feel very… preoccupied, I suppose, like I’m negotiating with myself over every bite, but that’s not quite the same thing. “Out of control” makes me think of someone eating without being able to stop, and I don’t really have that experience, at least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nAlthough it’s hard, because food situations can feel chaotic even when nothing dramatic is happening. Like at departmental lunches, everyone’s balancing plates and talking about grant deadlines, and I’m trying to work out whether I’ve taken too much or too little, and then I miss half the conversation. But that’s more anxiety and calculation than losing control, I think.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you are eating much more rapidly than normal during these times when you do eat?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / biological_scientist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed.

When I do eat, it’s usually quite deliberate, almost slow, because I’m thinking about it too much. I’m aware of how things look on the plate, and whether anyone’s watching, and—this is silly—but even the order I eat things in can start to feel important. Like if there’s a salad, I’ll find myself separating bits out, and then I’m suddenly remembering this old lab protocol where we had to separate samples into these tiny fractions and it took forever, which is not relevant, sorry.

So no, I wouldn’t say I eat much more rapidly than normal. If anything, I’m probably too aware of it to do that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yes—well, I was sent by occupational health, mostly. I think they’re concerned because I’ve got into this routine where I sort of push breakfast and lunch out of the way when I’m in the lab, or if I’m helping with exhibition work—time gets very odd in those settings, you know, everything is timed except eating, apparently.\n\nAnd then when I do finally eat, I tend to keep it… small. Controlled. I suppose that’s the word they used, or maybe I did. It just feels easier that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number, like in the lab when a reading fluctuates a bit and you’re not supposed to overinterpret it—though of course sometimes a tiny fluctuation is the whole point, depending on the assay.\n\nBut with my weight, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp panic. Like I’ve slipped, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. Eating feels tied to that, so yes… I do worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. That word feels very… imprecise, actually. In science we’d have to define “excessive” against some sort of baseline, wouldn’t we? Like excessive for me, or excessive for a normal meal, or excessive compared with what occupational health thinks people should eat during a workday?\n\nSometimes if I’ve gone a long time without eating, I might have something and then feel like it was too much afterward, even if—objectively, perhaps—it wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of deliberately making myself vomit after eating.\n\nI’ve felt nauseous before, but that can happen in the lab too—some of the fixatives, or even just strong smells, there was this one time with an incubator that had gone wrong and everyone pretended they were fine but we absolutely were not. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t make myself sick after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Not in a formal way, like I’ve made a list and laminated it or anything—though in the lab we do laminate everything, which is probably why my mind goes there.\n\nIt’s more that occupational health sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or exhibition work if I’m helping with that, and then when I finally do eat, I keep it to something very small and controlled. Something I can account for, I suppose. So… yes, certain foods, or at least certain very limited sorts of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose “significant” depends on what you’re comparing it to. My clothes are looser, which is hard to ignore because lab coats hide quite a lot, but trousers don’t, really. And the numbers have been… drifting down.\n\nI know I should probably sound more concerned about that. Part of me is. But another part of me sees it as evidence that I’m staying focused, if that makes sense—like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Which sounds a bit strange now that I’ve said it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body and emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is very loud.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny amount, even something that could just be water or—sorry, I know from a biology point of view bodies fluctuate, I’m not completely irrational about the mechanisms—but it still feels like this sharp panic. Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.\n\nIt tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.\n\nBy the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods where you eat a normal amount of food without feeling the need to restrict afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.\n\nBut honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. That’s partly why occupational health got involved, I suppose. So a “normal amount” without needing to compensate or tighten things up afterward… no, that doesn’t really happen much.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way you mean, probably.\n\nI mean, “large amount” is a bit hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s normal is probably not very reliable at the moment. Sometimes at conferences they put out those trays of pastries and fruit and everyone sort of hovers around them pretending they’re only there for coffee, and I’ll think about whether taking one thing looks strange or not taking one looks strange, which is exhausting and also ridiculous because no one is watching that closely.\n\nBut episodes where I suddenly eat a lot in a short time… I haven’t really noticed that as a regular thing. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is, though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIt’s not always some dramatic thought, like, “I can’t gain weight,” running through my head. It’s more immediate than that. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this sharp panic—like I’ve let something slip, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked very hard to keep. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about numbers, and whether the scale was on the same tile of the bathroom floor, because the tiles in my flat are slightly uneven—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant medical issues or health problems as a result of your weight loss?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said they’re worried, yes. Occupational health, a couple of colleagues in that very carefully polite way people use when they don’t want to offend you.\n\nBut I don’t really feel like it’s a medical issue, if that makes sense. I’m still going into the lab, still running experiments, still keeping track of things. Last week I was doing this ridiculous sequence where one sample had to be checked every—sorry, that’s not really relevant. I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.\n\nSo I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. It doesn’t feel dangerous to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as losing control, exactly.\n\nThere are times when I feel very… preoccupied, I suppose, like I’m negotiating with myself over every bite, but that’s not quite the same thing. “Out of control” makes me think of someone eating without being able to stop, and I don’t really have that experience, at least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nAlthough it’s hard, because food situations can feel chaotic even when nothing dramatic is happening. Like at departmental lunches, everyone’s balancing plates and talking about grant deadlines, and I’m trying to work out whether I’ve taken too much or too little, and then I miss half the conversation. But that’s more anxiety and calculation than losing control, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are eating much more rapidly than normal during these times when you do eat?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nWhen I do eat, it’s usually quite deliberate, almost slow, because I’m thinking about it too much. Like if there’s a salad, I’ll find myself separating bits out, and then I’m suddenly remembering this old lab protocol where we had to separate samples into these tiny fractions and it took forever, which is not relevant, sorry.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I eat much more rapidly than normal. I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.\n\nSo I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. Like at departmental lunches, everyone’s balancing plates and talking about grant deadlines, and I’m trying to work out whether I’ve taken too much or too little, and then I miss half the conversation. But that’s more anxiety and calculation than losing control, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are eating much more rapidly than normal during these times when you do eat?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nWhen I do eat, it’s usually quite deliberate, almost slow, because I’m thinking about it too much. I’m aware of how things look on the plate, and whether anyone’s watching, and—this is silly—but even the order I eat things in can start to feel important. Like if there’s a salad, I’ll find myself separating bits out, and then I’m suddenly remembering this old lab protocol where we had to separate samples into these tiny fractions and it took forever, which is not relevant, sorry.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I eat much more rapidly than normal. If anything, I’m probably too aware of it to do that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever have times when you feel like you are eating an unusually large amount of food?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is asking about episodes of eating an unusually large amount of food, which relates to binge-eating behavior; the provided symptom profile does not include a binge-eating symptom item."}

Occupational health, a couple of colleagues in that very carefully polite way people use when they don’t want to offend you.\n\nBut I don’t really feel like it’s a medical issue, if that makes sense. Last week I was doing this ridiculous sequence where one sample had to be checked every—sorry, that’s not really relevant.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean, “unusually large” is a bit hard to judge, isn’t it? Because what looks large to one person might be normal to someone else, and then there’s context—like those conference buffet lunches where everyone piles things onto a plate because they’re trying to eat quickly between sessions, and the sandwiches always have those little labels that curl up at the edges…

Sorry. No, I don’t really have times where I’d say I’m eating a very large amount of food. I’m usually more aware of not wanting it to seem like too much, if anything.
