Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep hold of.\n\nAnd then I tell myself it’s about control, about keeping my emotions tidy, almost. But the fear of gaining weight is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, or I feel like I do when I’m keeping it very contained.\n\nIt tends to happen after I’ve already pushed breakfast and lunch out of the way—during lab runs, or sometimes exhibition work, because once you’re in the middle of setting something up it feels absurd to stop for a sandwich. Though people do, obviously. Sensible people do.\n\nBy the time I actually eat, I want it to be something small and predictable. Controlled, I suppose. That’s when it feels like I’ve got a handle on things. If it’s more open-ended than that, I get… uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods where you eat a normal amount of food without feeling the need to restrict afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean… I can sit with people and appear to eat normally, I think, especially if there’s some work thing around it—though work meals are always a bit odd, aren’t they, everyone pretending not to notice who’s taking the last sandwich.\n\nBut honestly, the pattern has been that I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs or exhibition work, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled. That’s partly why occupational health got involved, I suppose. So a “normal amount” without needing to compensate or tighten things up afterward… no, that doesn’t really happen much.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way you mean, probably.\n\nI mean, “large amount” is a bit hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s normal is probably not very reliable at the moment. Sometimes at conferences they put out those trays of pastries and fruit and everyone sort of hovers around them pretending they’re only there for coffee, and I’ll think about whether taking one thing looks strange or not taking one looks strange, which is exhausting and also ridiculous because no one is watching that closely.\n\nBut episodes where I suddenly eat a lot in a short time… I haven’t really noticed that as a regular thing. It’s more that I’m thinking about it too much beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is, though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIt’s not always some dramatic thought, like, “I can’t gain weight,” running through my head. It’s more immediate than that. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this sharp panic—like I’ve let something slip, or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked very hard to keep. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about numbers, and whether the scale was on the same tile of the bathroom floor, because the tiles in my flat are slightly uneven—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes. The idea of gaining weight is a big part of why I restrict.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant medical issues or health problems as a result of your weight loss?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said they’re worried, yes. Occupational health, a couple of colleagues in that very carefully polite way people use when they don’t want to offend you.\n\nBut I don’t really feel like it’s a medical issue, if that makes sense. I’m still going into the lab, still running experiments, still keeping track of things. Last week I was doing this ridiculous sequence where one sample had to be checked every—sorry, that’s not really relevant. I suppose my point is, I’m functioning.\n\nSo I know other people think the weight loss is serious, but I honestly think they may be overstating it a bit. It doesn’t feel dangerous to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as losing control, exactly.\n\nThere are times when I feel very… preoccupied, I suppose, like I’m negotiating with myself over every bite, but that’s not quite the same thing. “Out of control” makes me think of someone eating without being able to stop, and I don’t really have that experience, at least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nAlthough it’s hard, because food situations can feel chaotic even when nothing dramatic is happening. Like at departmental lunches, everyone’s balancing plates and talking about grant deadlines, and I’m trying to work out whether I’ve taken too much or too little, and then I miss half the conversation. But that’s more anxiety and calculation than losing control, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are eating much more rapidly than normal during these times when you do eat?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nWhen I do eat, it’s usually quite deliberate, almost slow, because I’m thinking about it too much. I’m aware of how things look on the plate, and whether anyone’s watching, and—this is silly—but even the order I eat things in can start to feel important. Like if there’s a salad, I’ll find myself separating bits out, and then I’m suddenly remembering this old lab protocol where we had to separate samples into these tiny fractions and it took forever, which is not relevant, sorry.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I eat much more rapidly than normal. If anything, I’m probably too aware of it to do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times when you feel like you are eating an unusually large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, “unusually large” is a bit hard to judge, isn’t it? Because what looks large to one person might be normal to someone else, and then there’s context—like those conference buffet lunches where everyone piles things onto a plate because they’re trying to eat quickly between sessions, and the sandwiches always have those little labels that curl up at the edges…\n\nSorry. No, I don’t really have times where I’d say I’m eating a very large amount of food. I’m usually more aware of not wanting it to seem like too much, if anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating in front of other people because you're worried about what they might think of your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’m still getting the main work done—deadlines, reports, the necessary meetings and all that—but I’ve stopped going to the lab lunches, mostly. And there are these informal meals for gallery planning, which used to be a nice way to talk things through, actually… the kind where someone brings up lighting or wall text over sandwiches, and then suddenly the whole plan changes because of one casual comment.\n\nI don’t really go to those now. I tell myself I’m busy or that I can catch up by email, but I think part of it is that I don’t want people noticing how I eat, or what I choose, or wondering about it. So I’ve become a bit more separate from people at work than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel like you are eating without being able to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I don’t really have that feeling of starting and then not being able to stop.\n\nIf anything, I’m usually very aware of stopping, or of how much is there, or whether I should leave some… though now I’m wondering if that sounds odd. It’s just not like losing control in that way.\n\nSorry, I’m trying to answer plainly. No, I haven’t really noticed episodes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious about losing control over what or how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not so much that I think I’ll suddenly eat huge amounts, but more that I’ll lose control of the rules I have for myself.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a tiny fraction—honestly, a number most people probably wouldn’t even register—I get this sharp panic. It feels like proof that I’m slipping, or that I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked really hard to keep. Which sounds a bit dramatic when I say it out loud, because scales are so fickle—water, time of day, even those old lab balances we used in undergrad had to be recalibrated constantly…\n\nSorry. Yes. I feel anxious about losing control if I think my weight is going up at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any episodes where you feel like you are eating a normal amount of food without later feeling the need to restrict or compensate?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can eat something and have it look normal enough, I suppose, depending on who’s watching. But in my head it doesn’t feel normal afterward.\n\nOccupational health probably sent me because I’ve got into this pattern where I push breakfast and lunch aside during lab runs, or when I’m caught up with exhibition work—install schedules are ridiculous sometimes, everyone thinks the labels are the simple part and then they take three rounds—and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small. Controlled.\n\nSo I don’t often have a meal and just… move on from it. There’s usually some adjustment around it, or I make sure it stays within very narrow limits.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time, which you would consider to be more than what most people would eat under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not large amounts, no. I’m trying to think what “large” would mean, because people’s portions vary so much. At conferences, for example, those buffet lunches are always odd—some people pile plates very high and then barely eat, and others go back twice because the first plate was mostly lettuce, and it’s hard to know what counts as “normal.”\n\nBut for me, no, I can’t really think of a time where I ate a lot in a short period and thought, “That was more than most people would eat.” I’m usually more… aware of the amount than that."
